    "oldString": "Our contributions: (1)~a systematic causal diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way factorial comparison that isolates four causal factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based causal exclusion via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification; this design pattern is in principle applicable to other auxiliary objective analyses (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \\citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ to 1.57$\\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \\S\\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline for future attention steering research: ThreadGate with seven loss formulations and four training configurations ruled out (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
    "newString": "Our contributions: (1)~a systematic diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \\citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ to 1.57$\\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \\S\\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline for future attention steering research: ThreadGate with seven loss formulations and four training configurations ruled out (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \\citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \\citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.\n\nA striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \\emph{not} affect. \\citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\\,=\\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n  \\caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}\n  \\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThreadGate combines per-layer MLP gate networks with a joint training objective: next-token prediction (NTP), an auxiliary GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), and a gate diversity loss (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). The final formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families; only MSE target with gate diversity converged stably. To diagnose the failure, we construct a five-way controlled comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) where each pair isolates one factor (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} serves a dual role: training strategy and exclusion tool that rules out gradient direction conflict.\n\nTraining succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The factorial comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~a systematic causal diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way factorial comparison that isolates four causal factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based causal exclusion via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification; this design pattern is in principle applicable to other auxiliary objective analyses (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \\citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ to 1.57$\\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \\S\\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline for future attention steering research: ThreadGate with seven loss formulations and four training configurations ruled out (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

### Tool result

_source line 2679 · 2026-05-22T09:12:13.229Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013ykELg5quJiKnChqg7w4nw`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause (\S\ref{subsec:steering}). Alternative hypotheses remain open: progressive attention drift unrelated to exposure bias, KV cache accumulation effects at long context lengths, or implicit label smoothing from the auxiliary loss could each contribute to the observed gap. Our evidence is consistent with exposure bias as the dominant factor, but does not exclude these alternatives. Split-softmax \citep{li2024measuring} offers a complementary inference-time approach that restructures attention computation rather than applying additive bias; unlike $\beta$-steering, it addresses the training-inference mismatch at the architectural level but cannot resolve exposure bias in auxiliary losses that require retraining.
4	
5	\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, linearly mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. Autoregressive GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the ${\sim}$31$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\ 1.36). GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\to$0.353), suggesting indirect improvement of all-token goal attention. The two paths are complementary: $\beta$\,=\,5 achieves the strongest GAR recovery but incurs 24\% PPL cost (1.43\,$\to$\,1.77); $\beta$\,=\,3 offers a more balanced tradeoff (PPL +9\%). Scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining. A second seed trained from scratch (rather than resumed from a standard-trained epoch) yields AR GAR\,=\,0.418 (gap 1.20$\times$), versus 5\% seed variance in standard ThreadGate training, suggesting that early scheduled sampling exposure---before the model consolidates teacher-forced attention patterns---may further compress the gap; systematic schedule ablation is deferred.
6	
7	\paragraph{Instruction retention under steering.} Across 18 system-prompt constraint types, $\beta$\,=\,3 steering increases content constraint compliance by +23.8\,pp (GAR-compliance $r$\,=\,0.200, $p$\,=\,0.007; McNemar $p$\,=\,0.077), providing exploratory evidence for downstream benefit pending multi-model confirmation.
8	
9	TRC analysis (\S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without disrupting hidden-state coherence. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested. Remaining directions include adaptive $\beta$ scheduling and layer-selective steering.
10	
```

    "newString": "compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\\ 1.36), consistent with exposure bias as a primary driver of the gap. GAR-Dongre also rises",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\nThe failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). Alternative hypotheses remain open: progressive attention drift unrelated to exposure bias, KV cache accumulation effects at long context lengths, or implicit label smoothing from the auxiliary loss could each contribute to the observed gap. Our evidence is consistent with exposure bias as the dominant factor, but does not exclude these alternatives. Split-softmax \\citep{li2024measuring} offers a complementary inference-time approach that restructures attention computation rather than applying additive bias; unlike $\\beta$-steering, it addresses the training-inference mismatch at the architectural level but cannot resolve exposure bias in auxiliary losses that require retraining.\n\n\\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, linearly mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. Autoregressive GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\\ 1.36). GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\\to$0.353), suggesting indirect improvement of all-token goal attention. The two paths are complementary: $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 achieves the strongest GAR recovery but incurs 24\\% PPL cost (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.77); $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 offers a more balanced tradeoff (PPL +9\\%). Scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining. A second seed trained from scratch (rather than resumed from a standard-trained epoch) yields AR GAR\\,=\\,0.418 (gap 1.20$\\times$), versus 5\\% seed variance in standard ThreadGate training, suggesting that early scheduled sampling exposure---before the model consolidates teacher-forced attention patterns---may further compress the gap; systematic schedule ablation is deferred.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruction retention under steering.} Across 18 system-prompt constraint types, $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 steering increases content constraint compliance by +23.8\\,pp (GAR-compliance $r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; McNemar $p$\\,=\\,0.077), providing exploratory evidence for downstream benefit pending multi-model confirmation.\n\nTRC analysis (\\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without disrupting hidden-state coherence. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \\citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \\citep{navon2022nash} remain untested. Remaining directions include adaptive $\\beta$ scheduling and layer-selective steering.\n",

GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\\texttt{q\\_proj}, \\texttt{v\\_proj}). The combined loss $\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}$ uses $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\\tau = 0.5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: gates without turn embedding (isolates turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\\pm0.001$, gap ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. IID evaluation uses teacher-forced UltraChat text; held-out uses 50 non-overlapping conversations. All four fine-tuned variants converge to within 0.75\\,pp on GAR-ours; PPL is indistinguishable. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID Evaluation}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out Evaluation}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), distribution shift is the failure mode.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nThe preceding sections established that all training-time interventions fail uniformly: exposure bias collapses autoregressive GAR regardless of gating or gradient management. We now test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR without retraining. We apply an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:steering}(a) presents initial results on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations. Low bias values ($\\beta \\in [0, 2]$) yield negligible GAR change (0.028\\,$\\to$\\,0.062) with stable PPL (${\\sim}$1.4). A sharp nonlinearity appears between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises only 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028).\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap that persists across all training strategies.",
    "newString": "where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a gap exceeding an order of magnitude that persists across all training strategies.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \\citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \\citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.\n\nA striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \\emph{not} affect. \\citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\\,=\\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n  \\caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}\n  \\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThreadGate combines per-layer MLP gate networks with a joint training objective: next-token prediction (NTP), an auxiliary GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), and a gate diversity loss (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). The final formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families; only MSE target with gate diversity converged stably. To diagnose the failure, we construct a five-way controlled comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) where each pair isolates one factor (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} serves a dual role: training strategy and exclusion tool that rules out gradient direction conflict.\n\nTraining succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The factorial comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~a systematic diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \\citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ to 1.57$\\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \\S\\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline for future attention steering research: ThreadGate with seven loss formulations and four training configurations ruled out (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

        "lines": [
          " ",
          " Gates are ineffective because NTP pressure pushes them toward identity (\\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}), and this is not caused by gradient direction conflict (\\S\\ref{subsec:pcgrad}). The remaining question: why do GAR improvements under teacher forcing fail to transfer to autoregressive generation?",
          " ",
          "-The 20--33$\\times$ GAR gap (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}) persists across all training strategies: the model learns the intended attention pattern on the training distribution but collapses under model-generated text. GAR-Dongre provides a revealing control: under standard teacher-forced training it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys the concentrated last-token attention pattern while leaving diffuse attention unaffected (scheduled sampling breaks this pattern; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
          "+The TF-AR GAR gap (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}) persists across all training strategies: the model learns the intended attention pattern on the training distribution but collapses under model-generated text. GAR-Dongre provides a revealing control: under standard teacher-forced training it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys the concentrated last-token attention pattern while leaving diffuse attention unaffected (scheduled sampling breaks this pattern; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
          " ",
          " The mechanism is exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled,arora2022exposure}: during training, the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. The statistical properties diverge, and the 2--7\\% gate attenuation (\\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}) cannot compensate. \\citet{he2019exposure} showed that language models exhibit token-level self-recovery from exposure bias, and our PPL results confirm this (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). The critical distinction is between primary and auxiliary objectives: NTP is robust because it is the same task at both training and inference time, while GAR optimizes a distributional property of the input prefix that has no counterpart under autoregressive generation. Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap} confirms teacher-forced GAR-ours remains near $\\tau = 0.5$ (7--8\\% decay, $N$\\,=\\,45), yet autoregressive GAR collapses for all variants (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}b).",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

          " ",
          "-\\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} showed that exposure bias selectively destroys GAR-ours (20--33$\\times$ gap) while, under standard training, GAR-Dongre remains unaffected. We test whether this selectivity extends beyond attention with an orthogonal hidden-state probe.",
          "+\\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} showed that exposure bias selectively destroys GAR-ours while, under standard training, GAR-Dongre remains unaffected. We test whether this selectivity extends beyond attention with an orthogonal hidden-state probe.",
          " ",
          " We define Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC) as the cosine similarity between hidden states at the last-token position of adjacent assistant turns. If exposure bias caused broad representational failure, TRC should show a comparable gap. It does not. For ThreadGate, teacher-forced TRC is 0.334 and autoregressive TRC is 0.332 (gap $= +0.002$). For LoRA-only, teacher-forced TRC is 0.349 and autoregressive TRC is 0.411 (gap $= -0.062$, reversed): autoregressive generation does not degrade hidden-state coherence. Neither checkpoint shows a TRC gap comparable to the 20--33$\\times$ GAR collapse.",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

    "oldString": "\\citet{li2024measuring} independently quantified instruction instability in multi-turn dialogs and proposed split-softmax---separating instruction from conversation tokens during attention computation---as an inference-time remedy. Our work adds systematic diagnosis (five-way controlled comparison + PCGrad exclusion), exposure bias attribution, and training-time mitigation via scheduled sampling; our $\\beta$-steering complements split-softmax as an alternative inference-time approach (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
    "newString": "\\citet{li2024measuring} independently quantified instruction instability in multi-turn dialogs and proposed split-softmax---separating instruction from conversation tokens during attention computation---as an inference-time remedy. Split-softmax restructures the attention mechanism itself and requires no retraining, but cannot address exposure bias in auxiliary losses that depend on training-time optimization. Our work adds systematic diagnosis (five-way controlled comparison + PCGrad exclusion), exposure bias attribution, and training-time mitigation via scheduled sampling; our $\\beta$-steering complements split-softmax as an alternative inference-time approach (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-turn attention degradation.}\nRecent work has established that LLMs systematically degrade across conversation turns. \\citet{laban2025lost} showed that model performance drops on tasks placed in later turns, even when the tasks are identical to earlier ones. \\citet{liu2026intent} traced a related failure to intent mismatch, where models progressively shift from the user's original intent as dialogue extends. MT-Bench-101 \\citep{bai2024mtbench101} provides fine-grained multi-turn evaluation across 1388 turn-level instances, revealing that capability varies substantially across task categories. \\citet{dongre2026attention} established the quantitative foundation for this line of work: they defined the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR) and showed that attention to system-prompt goal tokens decays by 27--48\\% across turns in 12 models, while residual stream probes retain goal information with AUC\\,=\\,0.98. We observe a steeper 70.8\\% decline on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). This dissociation between attention collapse and residual persistence motivates our gating intervention. Their work characterizes the phenomenon; ours attempts the first systematic cure and, in doing so, uncovers why lightweight interventions fail to transfer from training to inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruction stability.}\n\\citet{li2024measuring} independently quantified instruction instability in multi-turn dialogs and proposed split-softmax---separating instruction from conversation tokens during attention computation---as an inference-time remedy. Our work adds systematic diagnosis (five-way controlled comparison + PCGrad exclusion), exposure bias attribution, and training-time mitigation via scheduled sampling; our $\\beta$-steering complements split-softmax as an alternative inference-time approach (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Residual gating and adaptive depth.}\nReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero} showed that residual scaling shapes optimization dynamics. Subsequent work targets computational efficiency: LayerDrop \\citep{fan2020layerdrop}, Mixture-of-Depths \\citep{raposo2024mixture}, FlexiDepth \\citep{luo2025flexidepth}, and CALM \\citep{schuster2022calm}. ThreadGate applies residual gating to a different objective: recovering attention patterns in multi-turn dialogue.\n\n\\paragraph{Attention manipulation.}\nStreamingLLM \\citep{xiao2024streaming} identified attention sinks and proposed anchor token retention; \\citet{hsieh2024found} showed positional bias against middle-positioned information; \\citet{qiu2025gated} replaced softmax with a learned gate for sparse attention. These approaches intervene inside the attention mechanism, while ThreadGate operates on the residual stream. Our results suggest that neither pathway alone overcomes the distributional gap between teacher-forced training and autoregressive inference.\n\n\\paragraph{Multi-task gradient conflict.}\nGradNorm \\citep{chen2018gradnorm}, PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, CAGrad \\citep{liu2021cagrad}, and Nash-MTL \\citep{navon2022nash} treat gradient conflict between competing losses as a root cause to be resolved through better optimization. We repurpose PCGrad as a diagnostic tool: eliminating directional conflict between NTP and GAR gradients does not improve GAR convergence. Gradient conflict is a symptom rather than the cause.\n\n\\paragraph{Exposure bias.}\nTeacher forcing creates a distributional mismatch between training and inference that compounds over sequence length \\citep{wiseman2016sequence,arora2022exposure}. Scheduled Sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled} introduced curriculum-based mixing of ground-truth and model-generated tokens. MIXER \\citep{ranzato2016sequence} combined token-level and sequence-level objectives, and Professor Forcing \\citep{lamb2016professor} used adversarial regularization to align hidden state distributions under both regimes. \\citet{he2019exposure} showed that autoregressive models exhibit self-recovery from exposure bias at the token prediction level. SEARNN \\citep{leblond2018searnn} proposed locally recurrent search-based training to avoid the mismatch entirely. This literature focuses on generation quality degradation in primary objectives. No prior work examines how exposure bias affects auxiliary objectives that target attention distributions, nor how it manifests in multi-turn dialogue settings. Our work identifies a distinct failure mode: auxiliary attention losses that converge under teacher forcing fail completely under autoregressive generation, even when NTP remains robust, because token-level self-recovery does not extend to distributional properties of attention.",

MT-Bench-101 \\citep{bai2024mtbench101} provides fine-grained multi-turn evaluation across 1388 turn-level instances, revealing that capability varies substantially across task categories. \\citet{dongre2026attention} established the quantitative foundation for this line of work: they defined the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR) and showed that attention to system-prompt goal tokens decays by 27--48\\% across turns in 12 models, while residual stream probes retain goal information with AUC\\,=\\,0.98. We observe a steeper 70.8\\% decline on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). This dissociation between attention collapse and residual persistence motivates our gating intervention. ```text
1	\section{Method}\label{sec:method}
2	
3	\subsection{Preliminaries}\label{sec:background}
4	
5	\paragraph{Goal Attention Ratio.}
6	We denote the task specification tokens in the system prompt as \emph{goal tokens} $\mathcal{G}$. \citet{dongre2026attention} defined the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR) for a model with $L$ layers and $H$ heads at turn $\tau$ as
7	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar}
8	  \text{GAR}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H \cdot |\mathcal{R}_\tau|} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{i \in \mathcal{R}_\tau} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)},
9	\end{equation}
10	where $\mathcal{R}_\tau$ is the set of response tokens at turn $\tau$ and $A_{i,j}^{(\ell,h)}$ is the attention weight from token $i$ to token $j$ at layer $\ell$, head $h$.
11	
12	\paragraph{Two GAR variants.}
13	\textbf{GAR-Dongre} is the original definition (Eq.~\ref{eq:gar}), averaging over all response tokens. \textbf{GAR-ours} restricts the measurement to the \emph{last token} of each assistant turn:
14	\begin{equation}\label{eq:gar-ours}
15	  \text{GAR\text{-}ours}(\tau) = \frac{1}{L \cdot H} \sum_{\ell=1}^{L} \sum_{h=1}^{H} \sum_{j \in \mathcal{G}} A_{t_\tau,\, j}^{(\ell,h)},
16	\end{equation}
17	where $t_\tau$ is the last token index at turn $\tau$. Attention at this position reflects the information drawn upon for continuation; we use GAR-ours as the optimization target for $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ (\S\ref{sec:loss}).
18	
19	\subsection{ThreadGate Architecture}
20	
```

### Tool result

_source line 2775 · 2026-05-22T09:17:46.795Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HK93WzbkswTuumrEicmqxF`</summary>

```text
15	
16	Training succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The factorial comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a gap exceeding an order of magnitude that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\S\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the ${\sim}$31$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
```

    "oldString": "The factorial comparison reveals",
    "newString": "The five-way comparison reveals",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \\citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \\citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.\n\nA striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \\emph{not} affect. \\citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\\,=\\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \\cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \\citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}\n  \\caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}\n  \\label{fig:overview}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nThreadGate combines per-layer MLP gate networks with a joint training objective: next-token prediction (NTP), an auxiliary GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), and a gate diversity loss (\\S\\ref{sec:method}). The final formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families; only MSE target with gate diversity converged stably. To diagnose the failure, we construct a five-way controlled comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) where each pair isolates one factor (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}). PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} serves a dual role: training strategy and exclusion tool that rules out gradient direction conflict.\n\nTraining succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The factorial comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a gap exceeding an order of magnitude that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad exclusion experiment eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These contrasts converge on exposure bias \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is a proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nOur contributions: (1)~a systematic diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way controlled comparison that isolates four factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based exclusion via gradient surgery \\citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \\citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ to 1.57$\\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (27$\\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \\S\\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline for future attention steering research: ThreadGate with seven loss formulations and four training configurations ruled out (\\S\\ref{sec:method}, \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

```text
9	  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}
10	  \caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}
11	  \label{fig:overview}
```

13	\begin{figure}[t]

15	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}
16	  \caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift: the 20--33$\times$ GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base), the failure is universal across all configurations.}
17	  \label{fig:gate-shift}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Jpdpp38GFawKB3wzRYS1Jm`</summary>

```text
3	\paragraph{Multi-turn attention degradation.}
4	Recent work has established that LLMs systematically degrade across conversation turns. \citet{laban2025lost} showed that model performance drops on tasks placed in later turns, even when the tasks are identical to earlier ones. \citet{liu2026intent} traced a related failure to intent mismatch, where models progressively shift from the user's original intent as dialogue extends. MT-Bench-101 \citep{bai2024mtbench101} provides fine-grained multi-turn evaluation across 1388 turn-level instances, revealing that capability varies substantially across task categories. \citet{dongre2026attention} established the quantitative foundation for this line of work: they defined the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR) and showed that attention to system-prompt goal tokens decays by 27--48\% across turns in 12 models, while residual stream probes retain goal information with AUC\,=\,0.98. We observe a steeper 70.8\% decline on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct (\S\ref{sec:experiments}). This dissociation between attention collapse and residual persistence motivates our gating intervention. Their work characterizes the phenomenon; ours attempts the first systematic cure and, in doing so, uncovers why lightweight interventions fail to transfer from training to inference.
```

</details>

    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "030bfbd9-f6cb-4a7e-bd9a-31117d7715c8",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "29e6f91f-56ce-4c4f-a507-800f9750d380",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "5f37e51e-0e6e-4c77-a03d-97ea44ae87db",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/turn_residual_gating/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
    "oldString": "the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap is universal.",
    "newString": "the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\nWe fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\\texttt{q\\_proj}, \\texttt{v\\_proj}). The combined loss $\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}$ uses $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\\tau = 0.5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: gates without turn embedding (isolates turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\\pm0.001$, gap ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. Teacher-forced (TF) evaluation uses IID UltraChat text and 50 held-out conversations; autoregressive (AR) evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn generated conversations. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap is universal. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), distribution shift is the failure mode.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nThe preceding sections established that all training-time interventions fail uniformly: exposure bias collapses autoregressive GAR regardless of gating or gradient management. We now test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR without retraining. We apply an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:steering}(a) presents initial results on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations. Low bias values ($\\beta \\in [0, 2]$) yield negligible GAR change (0.028\\,$\\to$\\,0.062) with stable PPL (${\\sim}$1.4). A sharp nonlinearity appears between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises only 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028).\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap is universal. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}",
          "+\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}",

The combined loss $\\mathcal{L} = \\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\lambda_\\text{GAR}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR} + \\lambda_\\text{div}\\mathcal{L}_\\text{div}$ uses $\\lambda_\\text{GAR} = \\lambda_\\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\\tau = 0.5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \\times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42; ThreadGate additionally with seed 123); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a controlled comparison isolating four factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: gates without turn embedding (isolates turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. A second ThreadGate seed (123) confirms stability: TF GAR-ours $0.504\\pm0.001$, AR GAR-ours $0.016\\pm0.001$, gap ${\\sim}$31$\\times$ (range 30.5--32.1$\\times$), with identical PPL. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. Teacher-forced (TF) evaluation uses IID UltraChat text and 50 held-out conversations; autoregressive (AR) evaluation uses $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn generated conversations. All fine-tuned variants converge under TF (0.75\\,pp spread); the 20--33$\\times$ TF-AR gap persists across all five configurations. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc c}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID (TF)}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out (TF)}} & \\textbf{AR} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7} \\cmidrule(lr){8-8}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 & 0.012 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.016 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.015 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.025 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), distribution shift is the failure mode.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nThe preceding sections established that all training-time interventions fail uniformly: exposure bias collapses autoregressive GAR regardless of gating or gradient management. We now test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR without retraining. We apply an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:steering}(a) presents initial results on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations. Low bias values ($\\beta \\in [0, 2]$) yield negligible GAR change (0.028\\,$\\to$\\,0.062) with stable PPL (${\\sim}$1.4). A sharp nonlinearity appears between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises only 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028).\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is a proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",
